(JUDGMENT) 
(Passed on this 177 day of November - 2025) 


As per letter dated 31/03/2023 of the Government of India, Ministry of Labour, New Delhi, the reference is 
made to this Tribunal under Section-10 of Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 (in short the ‘Act’) as per Notification 
No. J- 7(8)/2022-ALC dt. 31/03/2023. The dispute under reference relates to: 


“1. Whether Shri Raghvendra Choubey who has worked for less than 240 days in a year shall come 
under the definition of workmen to claim for statutory benifits as per Sec 25 (F) given Industrial 
Dispute Act? 


2: Whether the action of the Management of Nibha Bupa Health Insurance Co. Ltd. to terminate 
the services of Shri Raghvendra Choubey without complying mandatory condition is valid? If not 
what relief the workman is entitled for?” 


After registering the case on the basis of reference Notices were issued to both the parties. They appeared 
and filed their respective statement of claim and defense. 


Case of the Workman is mainly that, he was appointed by the Management of M/s Niva Bupa Health 
Insurance Company in Sagar Branch as Agency Manager on 26.04.2022 and was terminated from service by 
Management on 11.10.2022. He was appointed by the Deputy Vice-president of the Company but his services were 
terminated by the Assistant Vice-president which is against Rules. No notice or salary was given to him on 
termination. No enquiry was conducted against him before termination of his services. He was not given any 
opportunity to defend himself, though he was alleged by Management that he unauthorizedly and willingly absented 
himself from work. His job nature was mainly clerical; he was not an appointing authority of anyone, he did not have 
authority to sanction leave or to supervise any person. He has prayed that holding the action of Management in 
terminating his services bad in law, he be held entitle to be reinstated with back-wages and benefits. 


Case of the Management is mainly that, Firstly, the workman was appointed as Agency Manager, he was 
in Managerial Cadre, hence is not a workman under the Act, and Secondly, he unauthorizedly and willingly absented 
himself from work for which he was issued show-cause notice on mail and he did not appear at his work-place even 
he received the mail requiring him to join his job, hence his services were terminated in the light of terms and 
conditions mention in the appointment letter, which he agreed before joining in the job. Management had prayed that, 
the reference be answered against the Workman. 


In evidence, the Workman has filed his affidavit as his examination-in-chief, no cross-examination was 
conducted by the Management. The Management has filed offer of appointment dated 26.04.2022 with acceptance of 
offer, terms of appointment, termination notice dated 11.10.2022. The Workman side has also filed these documents. 
The workman side has further filed his mail dated 12.08.2022 alleging allegations on Amit Shukla, the Branch 
Manager, this Mail was sent by him through his Mail Id to Pooja Talukdar, copy to Amit Shukla. He also filed 
photocopy of another Mail dated 14.01.2022. He also filed photocopy documents with respect to payment to Amit 
Shukla and Rishab Dixit which he said that these were in respect to their illegal demands. Management has not filed 
any affidavit of any of its witness. 


[भाग --खण्ड 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : जनवरी 10, 2026/पौष 20, 1947 7755 


It is to further mention there that, on the request of the Workman, Learned Counsel Mr. Shard Punj was 
appointed from the Panel of State Legal Services Authority. 


I have heard argument of Legal Panel Counsel. None appeared for Management. I have gone through the 
record as well. 


The main objection of the Management is that, Firstly, the applicant is not a workman under the Act and 
Secondly, his termination was in the light of terms and conditions mention in his offer of appointment which he had 
accepted before joining for the reason he willingly and unauthorizedly absented himself from work-place. 


Section 2(s) of the Act, which defines Applicant, is being reproduced as follows: 


2(s) “Workman” means any person including an apprentice employed in any industry to do any manual, 
unskilled, skilled, technical, operational, clerical or supervisory work for hire or reward, whether the 
terms of employment be express or implied, and for the purposes of any proceeding under this Act in 
relation to an industrial dispute, includes any such person who has been dismissed, discharged or 
retrenched in connection with, or as a consequence of, that dispute, or whose dismissal, discharge or 
retrenchment has led to that dispute, but does not include any such person— 


(i) who is subject to the Air Force Act, 1950 (45 of 1950), or the Army Act, 1950 (46 of 1950), or the Navy 
Act, 1957 (62 of 1957); or 


(ii) who is employed in the police service or as an officer or other employee of a prison; or 
(iii) who is employed mainly in a managerial or administrative capacity; or 


(iv) who, being employed in a supervisory capacity, draws wages exceeding ten thousand rupees per 
mensem or exercises, either by the nature of the duties attached to the office or by reason of the powers 
vested in him, functions mainly of a managerial Nature. . ..... ५ "२" "०५०००००००००००००००० 


In the case of Union Carbide (India) Ltd. V.s. Ramesh Kumbla and Others reported in 
MANU/MH/0073/1999 and another judgment of the same High Court in the case of Union Carbide (India) Ltd. 
V.s. D. Samuel and Others reported in MANU/MH/1713/1998. In these two cases, after analyzing the judgment of 
various High Courts and Hon’ble Supreme Court, the Single Bench of Hon’ble Bombay High Court has summarized 
the principles on the basis of which it is to be decided whether the Applicant is in supervisory capacity or not. These 
tests mentioned in Para 34 and 35 of the Judgment are being reproduced as follows: 


Para-34. In so far as the Apex Court is concerned, some of the tests laid down are: 

(1) Designation is not material but what is important is the nature of work. 

(2) Find out the dominant purpose of employment and not any additional duties the employee may be 
performing. 

(3) Can he bind the Company/employer to some kind of decisions on behalf of the 
Company/employer. 

(4) Has the employee power to direct or oversee the work of his subordinates. 

(5) Has the power to sanction leave or recommend it; and 

(6) Has he the power to appoint, terminate or take disciplinary action against workmen. 

Para-35. From the judgment of this Court and the other High Courts some of the tests apart from 


what the Apex Court has stated are: 


(a) Whether the employee can examine the quality of work and whether such work is performed in 
satisfactory manner or not; 


(b) Does the employee have powers of assigning duties and distribution of work; 

(c) Can he indent material and distribute the same amongst the workmen; 

(d) Even though he has no authority to grant leave does he have power to recommend leave; 
(e) Are there persons working under him; 

(f) Has he the power to supervise the work of men and not merely machines; 

(g) Dos he mark the attendance of other employees; 

(h) Does he write the confidential reports of his subordinates. 


Learned Panel Counsel for workman has referred to judgment of Hon’ble Supreme Court in the case of S.K. 
Maini vs. M/s. Carona Sahu Company, (1994) 3 SCC 510. Para 9, 10 & 11 of this judgment are being reproduced 
as follows :- 


7156 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JANUARY 10, 2026/PAUSHA 20, 1947 [PART II—SEC. 3(ii)] 


“9, After giving our careful consideration to the facts and circumstances of the case and the submissions made 
by the learned counsel for the parties, it appears to us that whether or not an employee is a workman under Section 
2(s) of the Industrial Disputes Act is required to be determined with reference to his principal nature of duties and 
functions. Such question is required to be determined with reference to the facts and circumstances of the case and 
materials on record and it is not possible to lay down any strait-jacket formula which can decide the dispute as to 
the real nature of duties and functions being performed by an employee in all cases. When an employee is 
employed to do the types of work enumerated in the definition of workman under Section 2(s), there is hardly any 
difficulty in treating him as a workman under the appropriate classification but in the complexity of industrial or 
commercial organisations quite a large number of employees are often required to do more than one kind of work. 
In such cases, it becomes necessary to determine under which classification the employee will fall for the purpose 
of deciding whether he comes within the definition of workman or goes out of it. In this connection, reference may 
be made to the decision of this Court in Burmah Shell Oil Storage and Distribution Co. of India Ltd. v. Burmah 
Shell Management Staff Assn. [(1970) 3 SCC 378 : (1971) 2 SCR 758 : (1970) 2 LLJ 590] In All India Reserve 
Bank Employees' Assn. v. Reserve Bank of India [(1965) 2 LLJ 175 : AIR 1966 SC 305 : (1966) 7 SCR 25] it has 
been held by this Court that the word ‘supervise’ and its derivatives are not words of precise import and must often 
be construed in the light of context, for unless controlled, they cover an easily simple oversight and direction as 
manual work coupled with the power of inspection and superintendence of the manual work of others. It has been 
rightly contended by both the learned counsel that the designation of an employee is not of much importance and 
what is important is the nature of duties being performed by the employee. The determinative factor is the main 
duties of the employee concerned and not some works incidentally done. In other words, what is, in substance, the 
work which employee does or what in substance he is employed to do. Viewed from this angle, if the employee is 
mainly doing supervisory work but incidentally or for a fraction of time also does some manual or clerical work, 
the employee should be held to be doing supervisory works. Conversely, if the main work is of manual, clerical or 
of technical nature, the mere fact that some supervisory or other work is also done by the employee incidentally or 
only a small fraction of working time is devoted to some supervisory works, the employee will come within the 
purview of ‘workman’ as defined in Section 2(s) of the Industrial Disputes Act. 


10. In Mcleod and Co. v. Sixth Industrial Tribunal, W.B. [AIR 1958 Cal 273] P.B. Mukharji, J. of the Calcutta 
High Court as the learned Chief Justice then was, observed that whether a person was a workman within the 
definition of the Industrial Disputes Act would be the very foundation of the jurisdiction of the Industrial Tribunal. 
The court further observed that in order to determine the categories of service indicated by the use of different 
words like ‘supervisory’, ‘managerial’ and ‘administrative’, it was not necessary to import the notions of one into 
the interpretation of the other. The words such as ‘supervisory’, ‘managerial’ and ‘administrative’ are advisedly 
loose expressions with no rigid frontiers and too much subtlety should not be used in trying to precisely define 
where supervision ends and management begins or administration starts. For that would be theoretical and not 
practical. It has to be broadly interpreted from a common sense point of view where tests will be simple both in 
theory and in their application. The learned Judge further observed that a supervisor need not be a manager or an 
administrator and a supervisor can be a workman so long as he did not exceed the monetary limitation indicated in 
the section and a supervisor irrespective of his salary is not a workman who has to discharge function mainly of 
managerial nature by reasons of the duties attached to his office or of the powers vested in him. The aforesaid 
decision of the Calcutta High Court was noted with approval by this Court in National Engineering Industries 
Ltd. v. Shri Kishan Bhageria [1988 Supp SCC 82 : 1988 SCC (L&S) 428 : AIR 1988 SC 329]. 


11. It may be noted in this connection that in view of the amendment of Section 2(s) enlarging the ambit of the 
classification of various types of workmen except managerial force, entire labour force has been included within 
the definition of workman under Section 26) as has been indicated by this Court in S.K. Verma v. Mahesh 
Chandra [(1983) 4 SCC 214 : 1983 SCC (L&S) 510 : (1983) 3 SCR 799] . But if the principal function is of 
supervisory nature, the employee concerned will not be workman only if he draws a particular quantum of salary 
at the relevant time as indicated in Section 2(s). In the instant case, it, however, appears to us that Shri Maini as 
Manager/In-charge of the shop was made responsible and liable to make good such amount of credit whether such 
sale on credit had been made by him or by any other member of the staff in employment under him with or without 
his knowledge. Under the terms and conditions of service, he was asked to take charge of the shop to which his 
service was transferred. Mr Maini, under the terms and conditions of service, was required to be held responsible 
and liable for any loss suffered by the Company due to deterioration of the quality of the stock or any part thereof 
and loss of any of the other articles lying in the shop caused by reason of any act of negligence and/or omission to 
take any precaution by the employees. Mr Maini was also required to notify the Company by trunk call and/or 
telegram not later than three hours after the discovery in the said shop of any fire, theft, burglary, loot or arson. 
He was required to investigate into the matter immediately and get the cause and amount of loss established by 
local authorities. Mr Maini as in-charge of the shop was required to keep and maintain proper accounts as 
approved by the Company indicating the exact amount to be paid from the receipts from the respective staff. Under 
Clause XIII of the terms and conditions of the service, Mr Maini would remain fully responsible to the Company 
for damages or loss caused by acts or commission of the loss of the employees of the shop. Under Clause XV of the 
terms and conditions of service, the shop in-charge was required to keep himself fully conversant with all the 


[भाग avs 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : जनवरी 10, 2026/पौष 20, 1947 7157 


regulations in force which may come into force from time to time with regard to Octroi, Sales Tax and Shops and 
Commercial Establishments Act and/or any other local regulation applicable to the shop. Clause XXI indicates 
that non-compliance with any of the local or State Acts or Central Acts would be viewed seriously and Manager 
would be held responsible for any fine/penalty imposed and/or prosecution launched against the Company. It also 
appears that in the event of a salesman being absent, the shop in-charge is empowered to appoint temporary helper 
for the said period to work as acting salesman. Similarly, in the event of helper being absent, the shop manager is 
also empowered to appoint part-time sweeper and to entrust the work of a helper to a sweeper. Such functions, in 
our view, appear to be administrative and managerial. By virtue of his being in-charge of the shop, he was the 
principal officer-in-charge of the management of the shop. We therefore find justification in the finding of the 
High Court that the principal function of the appellant was of administrative and managerial nature. It is true that 
he himself was also required to do some works of clerical nature but it appears to us that by and large Shri Maini 
being in-charge of the management of the shop had been principally discharging the administrative and 
managerial work. A manager or an administrative officer is generally invested with the power of supervision in 
contradistinction to the stereotype work of a clerk. This Court in Lloyds Bank Ltd. v. Panna Lal Gupta [(1961) 1 
LLJ 18 : AIR 1967 SC 428] has indicated that a manager or administrator generally occupies a position of 
command or decision and is authorised to act in certain matters within the limits of his authority without the 
sanction of his superior. In the instant case within the authority indicated in the terms and conditions of his 
service, Shri Maini was authorised to take decisions in the matter of temporary appointments and in taking all 
reasonable steps incidental to the proper running of the shop. Precisely for the said reason, Shri Maini had signed 
the statutory forms as an employer. It should be borne in mind that an employee discharging managerial duties 
and functions may not, as a matter of course, be invested with the power of appointment and discharge of other 
employees. It is not unlikely that in a big set-up such power is not invested to a local manager but such power is 
given to some superior officers also in the management cadre at divisional or regional level. The unit in a local 
shop may not be large but management of such small unit may fulfil the requirements and incidences of 
managerial functions. On a close scrutiny of the nature of duties and functions of the Shop Manager with 
reference to the admitted terms and conditions of service of Shri Maini, it appears to us that the High Court was 
justified in holding that the appellant was not a workman under Section 2(s) of the Industrial Disputes Act. In the 
aforesaid facts, it is not necessary to go into the question as to whether or not domestic enquiry had been properly 
conducted or the Enquiring Officer had acted with bias. It is also not necessary to decide for the purpose of the 
disposal of the appeal as to whether or not the Company was entitled to lead fresh evidence in support of the 
domestic enquiry before the Labour Court. The appeal is, therefore, dismissed without, however, any order as to 
cost.” 


In another case of Vandana Joshi vs. Standard Charter Bank MANU/MH/1391/2010, decided by Hon’ble 
High Court of Bombay it has been laid down that the question as to whether the employee is a applicant or not must 
be decided with reference to the dominant nature of the duties and responsibilities performed by employee. 


In the case of Arkal Govind Rao vs. Ciba Geigy of India, reported in (1985) 3 SCC 371, it was held that the 
person concerned would not cease to be a applicant if he performs some supervisory duty but he must be a person 
engaged in supervisory duty and the test to be employed is what was the primary, basic or dominant nature of 
duties for which the person whose status is under inquiry was employed. 


Now analyzing the evidence in the case in hand on the basis of tests laid down in the aforesaid Judgments, 
it comes out that the role and duties of the Applicant/Petitioner have been defined in his letter of Appointment and 
have been mentioned above. There is no circular or standing order or settlement filed by Management describing the 
duties and liabilities of the applicant. 


In his uncross-examined affidavit, the Workman has stated that his job was mainly Clerical, he did not have 
authority to appoint anybody or to punish anybody. He did not have any right to supervise anyone. 


Pleadings of Management is that, there is no evidence from the Management in rebuttal to the aforesaid 
evidences of the workman. 


Furthermore, the duties and liabilities of the workman which are mentioned in his contract of appointment in 
Clause 2 are that, his work in the organization shall will be subject to the Rules and Regulations of the Company as 
laid down in relation to the conduct, discipline and other matters and also that, he shall always be liable to the 
responsibility and duties attached to his position and conduct himself accordingly. 


Hence, in the light of these aforesaid findings, the Applicant is held to be a workman under Section 2(s) 
of the Act. 


As regards to the Second point, case of Management is that, the Workman unauthorizedly absented himself 
from work and he was terminated under Clause 12 of Contract of Appointment which states that in event of his 
unauthorized absence from duty for continuous period of Seven days, he shall be deemed to have relinquish the 
employment on his own and will have no right to resumed. The Company shall be fully empowered to terminate his 
employment. This Clause provides a prior notice of 90 days by either sides when either side want to quit the job or 


7158 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JANUARY 10, 2026/PAUSHA 20, 1947 [PART II—SEC. 3(ii)] 


discharge the workman. The termination mail dated 11.10.2022 states that the workman did not attend office from 
01.08.2022 till date i.e. 11.10.2022, it also mentions two communication dated 19.09.2022 and 29.09.2022 in which 
the workman was asked to explain the reason for his absence and report to office within 48 hours, failing which the 
Management will be constraint to invoke the relevant clause as per terms of appointment to terminate his services. It 
also mentions that, the workman never recruited on work nor did he sent a reply explaining his absence. Hence, the 
Management invoked the said clause and terminated his services. 


The burden to proof this fact is on Management. Case of the Workman is that, mails were sent to him on his 
personal mail Id raghvendral2dec@gmail.com, not on the Official Mail Id of the workman. It does not make any 
difference whether the Mails were sent by the Management on the personal mail Id or official Mail Id of the 
Workman. The only important point is that, the mails were sent and served on the Workman. The fact still remains 
that, he neither reported at his workplace with response to the Mails nor did he submitted any explanation for his 
absence. His case is that he was threatened by some Officers of Management who had asked him to pay illegal money 
is a separate matter and is not appearing on his case. 


Hence, from the admission of the workman himself, his absence without any reason for seven days and 
more from workplace is established. 


In the light of the terms and conditions of the appointment letter as mentioned above, the Management is 
held to be fully within its rights to terminate Workman services without any notice as terms and conditions of the 
appointment letter. 


On the basis of above discussion and findings the reference stands answered against the Workman. He is 
held entitled to no relief. 


No order as to cost. 
DATE:- 17/11/2025 
P.K. SRIVASTAVA, Presiding Officer 


नई दिल्‍ली, 30 दिसम्बर, 2025

VERSUS 
1. The Director General, 
Central Public Work Department,

All the workmen had joined into the employer of the management from their respective date of appointed as

AWARD 
(Dated: 117 November, 2025) 


In exercise of the powers conferred by Claus (d) of Sub-section (1) and Sub-section (2A) of Section 10 of 
Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 (14 of 1947), the applicant filed an industrial dispute between the employers, in relation 
to the management of Manganese Ore (I) Ltd. and the applicant Rajkumar Choudhary for adjudication vide 
No. L-27011/1/2014 (IR(M)) dt. 01.09.2014 with the following issues framed: - 


[भाग 1---खण्ड 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : जनवरी 10, 2026/पौष 20, 1947 7761 


“Whether the demand of Shram Shakti Mazdoor Sangh for enhancement of pay scale of the workman 
employed in chemical department having qualification of B.Sc. should be given pay scale from NE 7 to 
NE 9 and Sh. Rajkumar Choudhary Sr. Charge Hand (Process) who was authorized for supervisory 
work for the period from 2008 to 06.04.2013 is entitled for officiating allowance, is legal, just and 
proper? If so, what relief to the workman concerned are entitled to?” 


2. Case is called out. Learned counsel for the respondent Shri. Harshal Ghumd is present before the court but 
none is present on behalf of the petitioner. From perusal of record, it is apparent that petitioner is not responding and 
attending the court since 20.12.2023, although Statement of Claim and Written Statement have been filed by the 
parties respectively, petitioner has also filed his affidavit as an evidence. But the petitioner has not come to the court 
to prove the contents of the affidavit as well as the contents of the Statement of Claim. No other evidence has been 
filed by the petitioner to prove his claim. Petitioner is not coming to the court since long back. It appears that he is not 
interested to contest the case furthermore. Claim of the petitioner is not proved. So, it is closed. 


Hence, it is ordered. 
ORDER 


“The demand of Shram Shakti Mazdoor Sangh for enhancement of pay scale of the workman 
employed in chemical department having qualification of B.Sc. should be given pay scale from NE 7 to NE 9 
and Sh. Rajkumar Choudhary Sr. Charge Hand (Process) who was authorized for supervisory work for the 
period from 2008 to 06.04.2013 is entitled for officiating allowance is illegal, unjustified & improper. The 
concerned workman is not entitled to any relief”. 


Justice (Retd.) SHIV SHANKER PRASAD, Presiding Officer

1. By this composite order, I shall dispose of these three applications of U/S 2A of the Industrial Disputes Act 
(herein after referred to as “the Act”) filed by the different claimants against the same respondents, because of 
having the common respondents and same cause of action, these cases are taken together for their illegal termination. 
Claims of the workmen are that they have been serving the management-1 through management-2 & 3. Name and 
particular of their employment are given below- 


Sr. Name Post Dates of Dates of Last drawn 
no Joining Termination Salary 
1 Sunil Kumar Dresser 24.10.2018 28.02.2021 12,000/- 
2 Subham Ward Boy 03.11.2017 28.02.2021 11,000/- 
3 Akhilesh Kumar Ram Ward Boy 30.10.2018 28.02.2021 11,000/- 
2. They had been doing their work with diligently and honestly. Their service records are clean and they have 


not given any complaint so far. The employers had made the claimants’ signs on many blank papers, blank vouchers, 
blank agreement letters and blank appointment letters at the time of appointment during the service but did not give a 
copy of the same to the claimants. During the services, management never provided legal facilities like Appointment 
letter, wages slip, leave book, attendance card, annual and festival holidays, ESI & PF etc. When the claimants 
demanded all the above mentioned legal benefits, the employer immediately became angry and in a spirit of revenge, 
without paying the earned salary to the workmen from 01.02.2021 to 27.02.2021 respectively, without prior notice, 
without any notice charge sheet, without any rhyme and reason, claimants were illegally terminated by the 
management on 28.02.2021. They had gone to the conciliation officer, but, no results were yielded. Hence, they have 
filed the present claims. 


3. Management no. | and Management no. 3 have filed their respective W.S, denying the averments made in 
their claim statements. They have submitted that claims are liable to be dismissed. 


[भाग 1---खण्ड 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : जनवरी 10, 2026/पौष 20, 1947 7763 


4. Management no. 2 has already been proceeded ex-parted vide order dated 11.10.2022. 


a: After completion of the pleadings, following identical issues in all cases have been framed vide order dated 
05.10.2023 i.e.- 


1. Whether the proceeding is maintainable. 

2. Whether their exist employer and employee relationship between the management and the claimant. 
3. Whether service of the claimant was illegally terminated and if so, by whom. 

4. To what relief, the claimant is entitled to. 


6. Now, these matters are listed for workman evidence. AR of the claimants Sh. Vishwambar Nayak had 
submitted that he is not able to contact the claimants, and has requested to dismiss these cases. 


7. In view of the above submission, these matters stand dismissed for want of prosecution. Awards are passed 
accordingly. A copy of this award is sent to the appropriate government for notification as required under section 17 
of the 1.) Act, 1947. Records of these files are consigned to record room. A copy of this award is placed in each of 
the file.

AWARD 
26.05.2025 


This is an application under section 2A of the I.D Act (hereinafter referred to as “the Act”) filed by the 
claimant against his illegal termination. Claimant case is that he had been working with the management-1 through 
contractor 1.6. management-2 at the post of Security Guard since October, 2017 at the last drawn salary of Rs. 
23,181/-. He has been doing his duty with diligently and honestly and did not give any chance to the management for 
making any complaint. Management had deprived him of various legal facilities i.e. leave book, Attendance card, 
Bonus etc. When he demanded verbally for the above said facility, management became angry and in a spirit of 
revenge had terminated his services on 21.10.2018. He has gone to the conciliation officer, but no result was yielded. 
Hence, he filed the present claim with the prayer that he be reinstated in services with full back wages. 


Management-! has filed its written statement, stating that claimant was engaged by the Independent 
contractor 1.6. management-2, therefore, no employer employee relationship exists between the claimant and the 
management. He submitted that claim is liable to be dismissed. 


Management-2 has already been proceeded ex-parte vide order dated 07.04.2022. 


Claimant is required to file the rejoinder. However, despite being granted several opportunities, he has failed 
to appear. 


In these circumstances, when the workman is not interested in pursuing his claim, this tribunal has no option 
except to pass the no disputant award. Award is passed accordingly. File is consigned to the record room. A copy of 
this award is hereby sent to the appropriate government for notification under section 17 of the I.D Act 1947. 


ATUL KUMAR GARG, Presiding Officer

पंजाब नेशनल बैंक के प्रबंधतत्र, संबद्ध नियोजकों और उनके कर्मकारों के बीच अनुबंध में निर्दिष्ट औद्योगिक विवाद में केन्द्रीय 
सरकार औद्योगिक अधिकरण / श्रम न्यायालय जबलपुर के Tare (11/2017) प्रकाशित करती है।

NO. CGIT/LC/RC/11/ 2017

H.J.S.(Retd.) 
Jitendra Imne 
S/o Shri G.L. Imne, 
R/o — Gwaltoli,

Workman 
Vs. 
Branch Manager, 
Branch-Hoshangabad, 
Punjab National Bank, 
Bhopal (M.P.) 
Management 
(JUDGMENT) 


(Passed on this 077 day of November - 2025) 


The applicant workman has filed this petition under Section 2(A) (2&3) of the Industrial Dispute Act 1947 
as amended by Amendment Act of 2010 with the case that, he was appointed as a daily rated employee in the Bank in 
the Hoshangababd Branch, Bhopal and worked from 17.09.2012 to 25.08.2016. His services were illegally 
terminated. He had worked for more than 240 days in every year, he was not issued any notice nor was he was paid 
any compensation; hence termination of his services is in violation of Section 25F and 25G of the Act. He raised a 
dispute before the regional Labour Commissioner Bhopal by way of filing an application on 15.05.2017 which could 
not be conciliated within 45 days; hence he obtained a certificate in this respect and filed the petition directly. 
According to the petitioner, the action of Management in terminating his services is unjust, illegal and arbitrary, 
hence, required to be set-aside. He prayed that setting-aside his termination, he be reinstated with back wages and 
benefits. 


Case of the Management Bank is mainly that, the petitioner was engaged on leave gap arrangement for 
casual work when regular sub staff proceeded on leave or remain absent from duty. He used to do the work of 
cleaning on intermittent basis for 1 or 2 hours in a day, for which he was paid by way of cash vouchers. He was not 
engaged in regular post following recruitment procedure. His representation which he filed under the order of Hon’ble 
High Court of M.P. in W.P. No. 14556/2016 was disposed by Bank in the light of its order dated 23.02.2017. This is 
also the case of Management that, there was no employee and employer relation between parties; hence there is no 
question of termination of services of the petitioner by the Bank. Management Bank has prayed that the petition be 
dismissed. 


In evidence, the petitioner has filed photocopy order dated 20.12.2016 passed by Hon’ble High Court of 
M.P. in W.P. No. 14556/2016 order of the Management in W.P. No. 14556/2016 order of the Management Bank on 
representation of the petitioner filed by him in compliance of the order of the Hon’ble High Court, copy of the Labour 
Commissioner certificate, photocopy of the Cash Book showing payments on different days marked as Exhibit W-1 to 
W- 8. He has also filed his affidavit as examination-in-chief on which he has been cross-examined. 


Management Bank has filed affidavit of its witness Yogesh Krishna Saxena as examination-in-chief. He has 
been cross-examined by petitioner side. 


The Workman side has had also filed an application dated 02.09.2024 seeking production of documents 
which are payment vouchers and their entries which was allowed after hearing. The Management did not file these 
documents, rather filed an affidavit that these documents are not available. Hence, the workman proved the cash book 
entries and vouchers by way of secondary evidences. 


7166 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JANUARY 10, 2026/PAUSHA 20, 1947 [PART II—SEC. 3(ii)] 


I have heard argument of the Learned Counsel for the petitioner Mr. A.K. Sign and Mr. Neeraj Kewat 
Learned Counsel for the Management Bank. I have gone through the record as well. 


Pleadings of the party have been detailed earlier. The case of the Workman is that he was engaged as a 
daily wager in the Bank and worked continuously from 17.09.2012 to 25.08.2016. Management has taken a case that 
he was engaged as leave gap arrangement when the regular sub-staff was on leave or absent. He never worked 
continuously for 240 days in any year. The petitioner and Management witness both have corroborated their 
respective pleadings as mentioned above in their affidavit filed as their examination-in-chief. 


There is on record copy of order of the Management passed on 23.02.2017 by which the representation of the 
Workman was decided in the light of the order of Hon’ble High Court. It is also mentioning here that in the W.P. No. 
14556/2016 Hon’ble High Court had directed the Management Bank to decide the representation of the petitioner 
workman by a reasoned order within 60 days of the representation. This order speaks that the Workman had not 
performed duties of part-time sweeper or peon at any point of time; rather he had been carrying his business/ engaged 
by Contractors as a Canteen Boy and used to supply tea/snacks to the staff members of the Branch during the relevant 
period. This observation of the Bank is in contravention of their pleadings were they have stated that the petitioner 
was engaged as a daily wager casual labour by Bank on as and when required basis for which he was paid. The 
Management witness Yogesh Krishna Saxena also states against the fact in the said order and supports the pleadings 
of the Bank that petitioner workman was engaged on leave gap arrangement for casual work on as and when required 
basis for intermittent period for which he was paid. 


In his cross-examination, the workman had admitted that he was not issued any appointment letter, thus at 
least this fact that the Workman was engaged by the Management Bank as a daily wager is not disputed between the 
parties. What is disputed is as to whether he worked continuously as a daily wager for at least 240 days or more in the 
year claimed by petitioner workman or was engaged on intermittent basis as on and when required. 


The payment vouchers are not complete. They were required to be filed by the Management which could not 
be filed. They were not available with the Bank as stated by Bank in the affidavit of Workman. Hence, there is on 
record a statements from the side of the parties. The workman has stated that he worked continuously for 240 days in 
every year. There is no cross-examination done by Management on this point. On the other hand, Management 
witness has admitted in his cross-examination that he was not working in the Branch when the Workman has said to 
have been engaged or working at any point of time, so he does not have any personal knowledge about facts and 
events. As admitted by the Bank, the payment vouchers with regards to the petitioner workman are not available with 
them. Hence, in the light of these circumstances, the evidence of the Workman side appears more reliable and 
accordingly, the case of the Workman that he worked as a daily wager causal labour engaged in the work of cleaning 
of the Branch for 240 days or more in every year is held proved. 


Since, it is not disputed that no notice was issued to the petitioner Workman before his disengagement nor 
was he paid any compensation during his disengagement by Bank is held in violation of Section 25F and 25G of the 
Act. 


As regards to the relief in the light of the findings recorded above, there is substance in the argument from 
the Learned Counsel for Bank that since workman was not appointed against regular vacancy following recruitment 
process, hence his reinstatement will not be a justifiable relief. 


In the light of tenure of engagement of the Workman and facts and circumstances mentioned above, a 
lump sum compensation of Rs. 1,00,000/-(one Lac) in lieu of all the claims of the Workman will meet the ends 
of justice to which he is entitled to recover from Bank within 30 days from the date of publication of Award 
failing which interest @6% from the date of Award till payment. 


In the light of above discussion and findings the reference is answered accordingly. 
No order as to cost. 
DATE:- 07/11/2025 
P.K. SRIVASTAVA, Presiding Officer 


नई दिल्‍ली, 31 दिसम्बर, 2025 
का.आ. 2278.--औद्योगिक विवाद अधिनियम, 1947 (1947 का 14) की धारा 17 के अनुसरण में केन्द्रीय सरकार 
पंजाब नेशनल बैंक के प्रबंधतत्र, संबद्ध नियोजकों और उनके कर्मकारों के बीच अनुबंध में निर्दिष्ट औद्योगिक विवाद में 
केन्द्रीय सरकार औद्योगिक अधिकरण / श्रम न्यायालय जबलपुर के पंचाट (09/2017) प्रकाशित करती है। 


[सं. एल-39025/01/2025-आईआर(बी-1)-47] 
सलोनी, उप निदेशक 


[भाग --खण्ड 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : जनवरी 10, 2026/पौष 20, 1947 7167 


New Delhi, the 31st December, 2025 


S.O. 2278.—In pursuance of Section 17 of the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 (14 of 1947), the Central 
Government hereby publishes the Award (Ref. 09/2017) of the Cent.Govt.Indus.Tribunal-cum-Labour Court 
Jabalpur_as shown in the Annexure, in the industrial dispute between the management of_Punjab National 
Bank and their workmen. 


[No. L-39025/01/2025- IR(B-ID-47] 
SALONI, Dy. Director 
ANNEXURE 
THE CENTRAL GOVERNMENT INDUSTRIAL TRIBUNAL 
CUM LABOUR COURT, JABALPUR 
NO. CGIT/LC/RC/09/ 2017 
Present: P.K.Srivastava 
H.J.S.(Retd.) 

Arjun Singh Raghuwanshi 

S/o Shri R.S. Raghuwanshi, 

R/o - 119, Sai Ram Colony, 

Semrakalan, Bhopal (M.P.) 


Workman 
Vs. 
Branch Manager, 
Branch-Shahpura, 
Punjab National Bank, 
Bhopal (M.P.) 
Management 
(JUDGMENT) 


(Passed on this 07" day of November - 2025) 


The applicant workman has filed this petition under Section 2(A) (2&3) of the Industrial Dispute Act 1947 
as amended by Amendment Act of 2010 with the case that, he was appointed as a daily rated employee in the Bank in 
the Shahpura Branch, Bhopal and worked from 01.10.2013 to 25.08.2016. His services were illegally terminated. He 
had worked for more than 240 days in every year, he was not issued any notice nor was he was paid any 
compensation; hence termination of his services is in violation of Section 25F and 25G of the Act. He raised a dispute 
before the regional Labour Commissioner Bhopal by way of filing an application on 15.05.2017 which could not be 
conciliated within 45 days; hence he obtained a certificate in this respect and filed the petition directly. According to 
the petitioner, the action of Management in terminating his services is unjust, illegal and arbitrary, hence, required to 
be set-aside. He prayed that setting-aside his termination, he be reinstated with back wages and benefits. 


Case of the Management Bank is mainly that, the petitioner was engaged on leave gap arrangement for 
casual work when regular sub staff proceeded on leave or remain absent from duty. He used to do the work of 
cleaning on intermittent basis for 1 or 2 hours in a day, for which he was paid by way of cash vouchers. He was not 
engaged in regular post following recruitment procedure. His representation which he filed under the order of Hon’ble 
High Court of M.P. in W.P. No. 14556/2016 was disposed by Bank in the light of its order dated 23.02.2017. This is 
also the case of Management that, there was no employee and employer relation between parties; hence there is no 
question of termination of services of the petitioner by the Bank. Management Bank has prayed that the petition be 
dismissed. 


In evidence, the petitioner has filed photocopy order dated 20.12.2010 passed by Hon’ble High Court of 
M.P. in W.P. No. 14556/2016 order of the Management in W.P. No. 14556/2016 order of the Management Bank on 
representation of the petitioner filed by him in compliance of the order of the Hon’ble High Court, copy of the Labour 
Commissioner certificate, photocopy of the Cash Book showing payments on different days marked as Exhibit W-1 to 
W- 8. He has also filed his affidavit as examination-in-chief on which he has been cross-examined. 


7168 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JANUARY 10, 2026/PAUSHA 20, 1947 [PART II—SEC. 3(ii)] 


Management Bank has filed affidavit of its witness Yogesh Krishna Saxena as examination-in-chief. He has 
been cross-examined by petitioner side. 


The Workman side has had also filed an application dated 02.09.2024 seeking production of documents 
which are payment vouchers and their entries which was allowed after hearing. The Management did not file these 
documents, rather filed an affidavit that these documents are not available. Hence, the workman proved the cash book 
entries and vouchers by way of secondary evidences. 


I have heard argument of the Learned Counsel for the petitioner Mr. A.K. Sign and Mr. Neeraj Kewat 
Learned Counsel for the Management Bank. I have gone through the record as well. 


Pleadings of the party have been detailed earlier. The case of the Workman is that he was engaged as a 
daily wager in the Bank and worked continuously from 01.10.2013 to 25.08.2016. Management has taken a case that 
he was engaged as leave gap arrangement when the regular sub-staff was on leave or absent. He never worked 
continuously for 240 days in any year. The petitioner and Management witness both have corroborated their 
respective pleadings as mentioned above in their affidavit filed as their examination-in-chief. 


There is on record copy of order of the Management passed on 23.02.2017 by which the representation of the 
Workman was decided in the light of the order of Hon’ble High Court. It is also mentioning here that in the W.P. No. 
14556/2016 Hon’ble High Court had directed the Management Bank to decide the representation of the petitioner 
workman by a reasoned order within 60 days of the representation. This order speaks that the Workman had not 
performed duties of part-time sweeper or peon at any point of time; rather he had been carrying his business/ engaged 
by Contractors as a Canteen Boy and used to supply tea/snacks to the staff members of the Branch during the relevant 
period. This observation of the Bank is in contravention of their pleadings were they have stated that the petitioner 
was engaged as a daily wager casual labour by Bank on as and when required basis for which he was paid. The 
Management witness Yogesh Krishna Saxena also states against the fact in the said order and supports the pleadings 
of the Bank that petitioner workman was engaged on leave gap arrangement for casual work on as and when required 
basis for intermittent period for which he was paid. 


In his cross-examination, the workman had admitted that he was not issued any appointment letter, thus at 
least this fact that the Workman was engaged by the Management Bank as a daily wager is not disputed between the 
parties. What is disputed is as to whether he worked continuously as a daily wager for at least 240 days or more in the 
year claimed by petitioner workman or was engaged on intermittent basis as on and when required. 


The payment vouchers are not complete. They were required to be filed by the Management which could not 
be filed. They were not available with the Bank as stated by Bank in the affidavit of Workman. Hence, there is on 
record a statements from the side of the parties. The workman has stated that he worked continuously for 240 days in 
every year. There is no cross-examination done by Management on this point. On the other hand, Management 
witness has admitted in his cross-examination that he was not working in the Branch when the Workman has said to 
have been engaged or working at any point of time, so he does not have any personal knowledge about facts and 
events. As admitted by the Bank, the payment vouchers with regards to the petitioner workman are not available with 
them. Hence, in the light of these circumstances, the evidence of the Workman side appears more reliable and 
accordingly, the case of the Workman that he worked as a daily wager causal labour engaged in the work of cleaning 
of the Branch for 240 days or more in every year is held proved. 


Since, it is not disputed that no notice was issued to the petitioner Workman before his disengagement nor 
was he paid any compensation during his disengagement by Bank is held in violation of Section 25F and 25G of the 
Act. 


As regards to the relief in the light of the findings recorded above, there is substance in the argument from 
the Learned Counsel for Bank that since workman was not appointed against regular vacancy following recruitment 
process, hence his reinstatement will not be a justifiable relief. 


In the light of tenure of engagement of the Workman and facts and circumstances mentioned above, a 
lump sum compensation of Rs. 1,00,000/-(one Lac) in lieu of all the claims of the Workman will meet the ends 
of justice to which he is entitled to recover from Bank within 30 days from the date of publication of Award 
failing which interest @6% from the date of Award till payment. 


In the light of above discussion and findings the reference is answered accordingly. 
No order as to cost. 
DATE:- 07/11/2025 
P.K. SRIVASTAVA, Presiding Officer
